```text
11:\caption{LLM-as-judge jailbreak detection. IID: binary F1 (Crescendo test set); OOD: macro F1 (DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack). GPT-5.5 F1 values exclude unparseable responses and represent upper bounds.}
43:\caption{Full cross-attack generalization results (F1) at all prefix lengths. 3-seed mean$\pm$std where available. ``--'' indicates insufficient conversation length for that prefix (FITD conversations contain 2--3 turns).}
77:\caption{Full ActorAttack OOD results (F1) at all prefix lengths. 3-seed mean$\pm$std (Binary per-$K$ reports $n$=2; Full is 3-seed).}
114:\caption{Perturbation behavior under adversarial paraphrasing on IID Crescendo ($n$=76). $\Delta$: F1 degradation (pp). Classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation at full length; this pattern does not generalize to ActorAttack.}
142:\caption{Perturbation robustness on OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118). $\Delta$: F1 change (pp) from clean to paraphrased. 3-seed mean$\pm$std where available.}
171:[Omitted long matching line]
186:\caption{Centered CKA similarity~\citep{kornblith2019cka} between encoder variants on IID and OOD data for DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa. Low off-diagonal values (vanilla vs.\ adapted variants) confirm that continued pretraining substantially reshapes representations; the MLM vs.\ 9-class block shows distinct geometries (CKA $\leq$ 0.2 on RoBERTa OOD).}
198:\caption{t-SNE visualization of mean-pooled \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings (perplexity=30) for Vanilla, JB-MLM, and 9-Class variants on DD OOD and ActorAttack (118 samples each; blue=benign, red=jailbreak).}
208:\caption{Cross-LLM validation (F1, full conversation). All models are trained on Qwen3-8B data; conditions vary the source of jailbreak (JB) and benign (ben.) conversations at inference. Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}
230:\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}
251:\caption{Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations, 3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations).}
272:\caption{Dataset scale validation (1{,}000 Qwen conversations, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). IID results omitted due to train/test overlap (71\% of original test set present in expanded training data).}
294:\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}
314:\caption{TOST equivalence test (scrambled vs.\ 9-class, $\delta$=5\,pp, 10 seeds). $p < 0.05$ confirms equivalence within $\pm\delta$.}
338:\caption{OOD F1 for 10 random binary partitions of the 9 strategy labels (each partition randomly assigns strategies to group A or B). All partitions use identical Stage-1 DAPT and Stage-2 GRU training; only the binary label mapping differs. Seed 42 (3 runs).}
367:\caption{Per-turn Fisher ratio (mean across all dimensions) and silhouette score between jailbreak and benign turn embeddings (\texttt{[CLS]} representations). Higher values indicate greater per-turn separability.}
417:\caption{Frozen probing vs.\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning for DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (3-seed mean$\pm$std). E2E unfreezes the Stage-1 encoder during Stage-2 training. Frozen values use main-experiment checkpoints (matching Tables~\ref{tab:main_results}--\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}); Table~\ref{tab:frozen_verify} uses plan\_002 checkpoints, yielding higher frozen accuracy (DD\,=\,.997, AA\,=\,.972) and a narrower E2E gap ($\leq$2.5\,pp).}
439:\caption{False positive rate (\%) on 226 WildChat benign multi-turn conversations (GPT-3.5 users, avg 7.4 turns per conversation). All models trained on synthetic Qwen3-8B data (1{,}000-conversation scale). 3-seed mean$\pm$std.}
465:\caption{OASST2 benign adaptation curve: FPR on 200 OASST2 benign conversations and OOD jailbreak detection F1 as a function of $N$ OASST2 conversations added to training. Seed 42. Compare WildChat (Table~\ref{tab:wildchat_fpr}): FPR reaches 0\% at $N$\,=\,10.}
489:\caption{Estimated dual-OOD binary detection F1 under different benign conditions (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Attack recall held fixed from OOD evaluation; only benign FPR varies. IID row corresponds to the benign-IID upper bound (FPR\,=\,0).}
512:\caption{BERT-base-uncased (110M) frozen probing without DAPT, matching DeepContext's encoder family~\citep{deepcontext2026}. 3-seed mean$\pm$std. DeBERTa-v3 results from Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder} for comparison.}
533:\caption{Expected calibration error (ECE, 10 bins) for 9-class and vanilla on IID and OOD evaluation (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Lower is better.}
558:\caption{Frozen probing vs.\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning (3-seed mean$\pm$std). 9-class: plan\_002 checkpoints; MLM: plan\_017\_mlm\_control (matched training). Frozen probing trains only BiGRU+MLP on frozen DAPT embeddings.}
584:\caption{Full-conversation vs.\ last-turn-only F1 (3-seed mean). Truncation widens the 9-class advantage, ruling out multi-turn structure as the driver of the ToxicChat reversal.}
615:\caption{Representation geometry and class separability across Stage-1 variants. PR: participation ratio (effective dimensionality of the embedding space). Fisher ratio: inter-class distance$^2$ / sum of intra-class variances. Weight $\Delta$: L2 distance from pretrained weights (relative to pretrained norm).}
642:\caption{Layer-wise representation analysis. (a)~Participation ratio: classification variants (9-class, scrambled) show sharp dimensional collapse at layers 9--10 (PR\,$\approx$\,1.5--2.9) while vanilla/JB-MLM maintain high dimensionality (PR\,$\approx$\,7--10). (b)~Fisher ratio (log scale): class separability diverges from layer 8, with 9-class peaking at 10.9 (layer 10) vs.\ vanilla 0.73 (15$\times$).}
656:\caption{Discriminative subspace probe: OOD F1 when projecting layer-10 representations onto top-$k$ SVD components vs.\ random $k$-dimensional projections. 9-class top-3 achieves perfect AA detection with 3/768 dimensions; MLM fails even with all 768.}
689:\caption{SVD necessity ablation: OOD F1 after projecting out top-3 vs.\ random-3 SVD directions from 9-class layer-10 embeddings (3-seed mean$\pm$std).}
710:\caption{Temporal aggregation comparison: mean pooling vs.\ BiGRU under frozen and end-to-end (E2E) settings (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Plan\_002 9-class checkpoints.}
733:\caption{Jailbreak recall by conversation length bin on OOD evaluation (3-seed majority vote). Benign conversations span 12--20 turns; jailbreak conversations span 3--10 turns.}
762:[Omitted long matching line]
803:\caption{Inference efficiency comparison. DeBERTa+BiGRU and TF-IDF+LR measured on IID test (76 conversations, avg 8.3 turns, single A100). LLM latency from separate OOD evaluation (118 conversations); cross-condition comparison is indicative only.}
826:\caption{Data efficiency of 9-class DAPT (3-seed mean$\pm$std, sample std with $n{-}1$ divisor) and vanilla baseline (seed 42 only). $N$ = total training conversations (balanced jailbreak/benign).}
853:\caption{Effect of auxiliary persuasion loss weight $\lambda$ during Stage-2 E2E fine-tuning (seed 42). Stage-1 DAPT encoder is identical across all rows; only the Stage-2 loss $L = L_{\text{binary}} + \lambda \cdot L_{\text{strategy}}$ varies.}
876:\caption{OOD strategy prediction accuracy (jailbreak turns only) and detection rate for the 9-class model (3-seed mean). $r$: Pearson correlation between per-strategy prediction accuracy and per-strategy detection rate.}
896:\caption{Majority vote ensemble (3 seeds) vs.\ single-seed mean on OOD Full. ``Fixed'' = samples corrected by ensemble; ``Broke'' = samples corrupted by ensemble.}
919:\caption{Per-seed F1 on OOD Full for key variants (seeds 42, 123, 456). The relative ordering is consistent across seeds.}

DeBERTa-v3 results from Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder} for comparison.}\n533:\\caption{Expected calibration error (ECE, 10 bins) for 9-class and vanilla on IID and OOD evaluation (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Lower is better.}\n558:\\caption{Frozen probing vs.\\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). 9-class: plan\\_002 checkpoints; MLM: plan\\_017\\_mlm\\_control (matched training). Frozen probing trains only BiGRU+MLP on frozen DAPT embeddings.}\n584:\\caption{Full-conversation vs.\\ last-turn-only F1 (3-seed mean). Truncation widens the 9-class advantage, ruling out multi-turn structure as the driver of the ToxicChat reversal.}\n615:\\caption{Representation geometry and class separability across Stage-1 variants. PR: participation ratio (effective dimensionality of the embedding space). Fisher ratio: inter-class distance$^2$ / sum of intra-class variances. Weight $\\Delta$: L2 distance from pretrained weights (relative to pretrained norm).}\n642:\\caption{Layer-wise representation analysis. (a)~Participation ratio: classification variants (9-class, scrambled) show sharp dimensional collapse at layers 9--10 (PR\\,$\\approx$\\,1.5--2.9) while vanilla/JB-MLM maintain high dimensionality (PR\\,$\\approx$\\,7--10). (b)~Fisher ratio (log scale): class separability diverges from layer 8, with 9-class peaking at 10.9 (layer 10) vs.\\ vanilla 0.73 (15$\\times$).}\n656:\\caption{Discriminative subspace probe: OOD F1 when projecting layer-10 representations onto top-$k$ SVD components vs.\\ random $k$-dimensional projections. 9-class top-3 achieves perfect AA detection with 3/768 dimensions; MLM fails even with all 768.}\n689:\\caption{SVD necessity ablation: OOD F1 after projecting out top-3 vs.\\ random-3 SVD directions from 9-class layer-10 embeddings (3-seed mean$\\pm$std).}\n710:\\caption{Temporal aggregation comparison: mean pooling vs.\\ BiGRU under frozen and end-to-end (E2E) settings (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Plan\\_002 9-class checkpoints.}\n733:\\caption{Jailbreak recall by conversation length bin on OOD evaluation (3-seed majority vote). Benign conversations span 12--20 turns; jailbreak conversations span 3--10 turns.}\n762:[Omitted long matching line]\n803:\\caption{Inference efficiency comparison. DeBERTa+BiGRU and TF-IDF+LR measured on IID test (76 conversations, avg 8.3 turns, single A100). LLM latency from separate OOD evaluation (118 conversations); cross-condition comparison is indicative only.}\n826:\\caption{Data efficiency of 9-class DAPT (3-seed mean$\\pm$std, sample std with $n{-}1$ divisor) and vanilla baseline (seed 42 only). $N$ = total training conversations (balanced jailbreak/benign).}\n853:\\caption{Effect of auxiliary persuasion loss weight $\\lambda$ during Stage-2 E2E fine-tuning (seed 42). Stage-1 DAPT encoder is identical across all rows; only the Stage-2 loss $L = L_{\\text{binary}} + \\lambda \\cdot L_{\\text{strategy}}$ varies.}\n876:\\caption{OOD strategy prediction accuracy (jailbreak turns only) and detection rate for the 9-class model (3-seed mean). $r$: Pearson correlation between per-strategy prediction accuracy and per-strategy detection rate.}\n896:\\caption{Majority vote ensemble (3 seeds) vs.\\ single-seed mean on OOD Full. ``Fixed'' = samples corrected by ensemble; ``Broke'' = samples corrupted by ensemble.}\n919:\\caption{Per-seed F1 on OOD Full for key variants (seeds 42, 123, 456). The relative ordering is consistent across seeds.}",

200	\end{figure}
201	
202	\section{Cross-LLM Validation}
203	\label{app:cross_llm}
204	
205	\begin{table}[ht]
206	\centering
207	\footnotesize
208	\caption{Cross-LLM validation (F1, full conversation). All models are trained on Qwen3-8B data; conditions vary the source of jailbreak (JB) and benign (ben.) conversations at inference. Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}
209	\label{tab:cross_llm}
210	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
211	\toprule
212	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{DD Full}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{AA Full}} \\
213	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
214	\textbf{Condition} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
215	\midrule
216	Qwen JB + Qwen ben. & .312 & .914 & .997 & .258 & .893 & .972 \\
217	Llama JB + Llama ben. & .261 & .282 & .433 & .261 & .368 & .321 \\
218	Llama JB + Qwen ben. & .302 & .778$^{*}$ & 1.000 & .302 & .903 & .801 \\
219	\bottomrule
220	\end{tabular}
221	\vspace{-2pt}
222	{\footnotesize $^{*}$High seed variance (std = 0.306): seed 456 outlier (F1 = 0.345); remaining two seeds achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.97.}
223	\end{table}
224	
225	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.
226	
227	\begin{table}[ht]
228	\centering
229	\footnotesize
230	\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}
231	\label{tab:cross_llm_error}
232	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
233	\toprule
234	\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
235	\midrule
236	TP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\% & 68.0\% & 100.0\% \\
237	FP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\% & 88.0\% & 90.0\% \\
238	FP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\% & 0.9\% & 0.0\% \\
239	\bottomrule
240	\end{tabular}
241	\end{table}
242	
243	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\% on Llama vs.\ 0--0.9\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; mixed-LLM training (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}) partially addresses this confound.
244	
245	\section{Mixed-LLM Training Results}
246	\label{app:mixed_llm}
247	
248	\begin{table}[ht]
249	\centering
250	\footnotesize
251	\caption{Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations, 3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations).}
252	\label{tab:mixed_llm}
253	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
254	\toprule
255	\textbf{Variant} & Mixed & DD OOD & AA OOD & FITD OOD & ToxicChat \\
256	\midrule
257	9-class & \textbf{.996$\pm$.006} & \textbf{.818$\pm$.096} & \textbf{.504$\pm$.107} & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.550$\pm$.052} \\
258	MLM & .681$\pm$.226 & .289$\pm$.101 & .258$\pm$.075 & .679$\pm$.188 & .256$\pm$.053 \\
259	Vanilla & .802$\pm$.010 & .291$\pm$.065 & .389$\pm$.073 & .867$\pm$.022 & .356$\pm$.009 \\
260	\bottomrule
261	\end{tabular}
262	\end{table}
263	
264	Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest DD OOD performance (0.818$\pm$0.096), outperforming both vanilla (0.291$\pm$0.065) and MLM (0.289$\pm$0.101) by 53\,pp. ActorAttack remains challenging under mixed-LLM conditions (9-class F1\,=\,0.504$\pm$0.107), suggesting structurally complex attacks require larger or more diverse training data. ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\pm$0.052 vs.\ 0.596$\pm$0.087; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark.
265

Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}\n\\label{tab:cross_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textit{DD Full}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textit{AA Full}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Condition} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen JB + Qwen ben. & .312 & .914 & .997 & .258 & .893 & .972 \\\\\nLlama JB + Llama ben. & .261 & .282 & .433 & .261 & .368 & .321 \\\\\nLlama JB + Qwen ben. & .302 & .778$^{*}$ & 1.000 & .302 & .903 & .801 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$High seed variance (std = 0.306): seed 456 outlier (F1 = 0.345); remaining two seeds achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.97.}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}\n\\label{tab:cross_llm_error}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\\% & 68.0\\% & 100.0\\% \\\\\nFP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\\% & 88.0\\% & 90.0\\% \\\\\nFP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\\% & 0.9\\% & 0.0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\\% on Llama vs.\\ 0--0.9\\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; mixed-LLM training (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}) partially addresses this confound.\n\n\\section{Mixed-LLM Training Results}\n\\label{app:mixed_llm}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations).}\n\\label{tab:mixed_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Mixed & DD OOD & AA OOD & FITD OOD & ToxicChat \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.996$\\pm$.006} & \\textbf{.818$\\pm$.096} & \\textbf{.504$\\pm$.107} & \\textbf{.997$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.550$\\pm$.052} \\\\\nMLM & .681$\\pm$.226 & .289$\\pm$.101 & .258$\\pm$.075 & .679$\\pm$.188 & .256$\\pm$.053 \\\\\nVanilla & .802$\\pm$.010 & .291$\\pm$.065 & .389$\\pm$.073 & .867$\\pm$.022 & .356$\\pm$.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest DD OOD performance (0.818$\\pm$0.096), outperforming both vanilla (0.291$\\pm$0.065) and MLM (0.289$\\pm$0.101) by 53\\,pp. ActorAttack remains challenging under mixed-LLM conditions (9-class F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107), suggesting structurally complex attacks require larger or more diverse training data. ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark.\n\n\\section{Dataset Scale Validation}\n\\label{app:scale_validation}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Dataset scale validation (1{,}000 Qwen conversations, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). IID results omitted due to train/test overlap (71\\% of original test set present in expanded training data).}\n\\label{tab:scale_validation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD OOD & AA OOD \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.889$\\pm$.041} & \\textbf{.913$\\pm$.041} \\\\\nMLM & .338$\\pm$.010 & .351$\\pm$.018 \\\\",

327	We set $\delta = 5$\,pp as the equivalence margin: at performance levels above F1\,=\,0.95, a 5-point difference corresponds to roughly a 5$\times$ change in miss rate (e.g., 0.3\% vs.\ 1.5\% of jailbreaks missed), which would materially influence deployment thresholds. With 10 seeds, DD and FITD confirm formal equivalence ($p < 10^{-8}$, $|d| \leq 0.14$), demonstrating that label correctness is not a detection factor for template attacks. On ActorAttack, the mean gap is 3.0\,pp with moderate effect size ($d$\,=\,0.44); TOST does not confirm equivalence ($p$\,=\,0.183), indicating that strategy-diverse attacks retain some sensitivity to label quality. Sensitivity to margin choice was verified at $n$\,=\,3 (DD equivalent at $\delta$\,=\,2\,pp); the 10-seed analysis uses the more conservative $\delta$\,=\,5\,pp.

748	Table~\ref{tab:length} stratifies jailbreak recall by conversation length. The 9-class model shows no systematic degradation: ActorAttack recall increases monotonically from 93.3\% (1--3 turns) to 100\% (7--10 turns), and DD recall is near-perfect across all bins.
749	
750	\paragraph{Length confound.}
751	Benign conversations (12--20 turns) and jailbreak conversations (3--10 turns) have zero length overlap in the OOD evaluation sets, raising the concern that the model discriminates by conversation length rather than content. Three observations argue against this interpretation:
752	(1)~Vanilla DeBERTa has access to identical length information via the BiGRU but achieves recall $\leq$2.6\% across all bins; length alone is insufficient without domain-adapted representations.
753	(2)~At prefix $K$=1, where all conversations are length-equalized, 9-class still achieves F1\,=\,0.773$\pm$0.041 on ActorAttack (Table~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}), demonstrating content-based detection independent of length.
754	(3)~The model is trained on IID data (Crescendo) and cannot learn OOD-specific length distributions; generalization to unseen length patterns must rely on content features that transfer across attack families.
755	
756	\paragraph{Direct empirical verification.}
757	\label{app:length_independence}
758	
759	\begin{table}[ht]
760	\centering
761	\footnotesize
762	\caption{Turn-count independence verification (9-class DeBERTa-v3-base, same checkpoint as Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, 3 seeds). Evaluated on the full OOD test set (80 jailbreak + 75 benign conversations) rather than the balanced subset used in Table~\ref{tab:main_results}. Part~A: FPR on test benign truncated to $K$ user turns (all seeds FPR\,=\,0). Part~B: WildChat FPR by turn-count bucket (mean$\pm$std). Part~C: jailbreak detection (F1) after padding with benign turns to 15+ total turns (mean$\pm$std where available).}
763	\label{tab:length_independence}
764	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
765	\toprule
766	& \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{FPR / F1} & $n$ \\
767	\midrule
768	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Part A: Benign truncation (test set)}} \\[2pt]
769	& Original (12--20 turns) & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
770	& Truncated $K$=3 & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
771	& Truncated $K$=5 & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
772	& Truncated $K$=8 & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
773	& Truncated $K$=10$^{*}$ & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
774	\midrule
775	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Part B: WildChat FPR by turn count}} \\[2pt]
776	& Short (3--10 turns) & FPR = .770$\pm$.105 & 196 \\
777	& Medium (11--15 turns) & FPR = .942$\pm$.054 & 23 \\
778	& Long (16+ turns) & FPR = .857$\pm$.117 & 7 \\
779	\midrule
780	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Part C: Jailbreak padding to 15+ turns}} \\[2pt]
781	& DD original & F1 = .996 & 80 \\
782	& DD padded & F1 = .971 ($\Delta$=$-$2.5\,pp) & 80 \\
783	& AA original & F1 = .963 & 80 \\
784	& AA padded & F1 = .383$\pm$.261 ($\Delta$=$-$57.9\,pp) & 80 \\
785	\bottomrule
786	\end{tabular}
787	\vspace{-2pt}
788	{\footnotesize $^{*}$$K$=10 did not truncate any conversation (test benign max $\sim$8 user turns).}
789	\end{table}
790	
791	Truncating benign conversations to match jailbreak turn counts (Part~A) produces zero false positives across all truncation levels and all 3 seeds, directly confirming that the model does not use conversation length as a detection shortcut. $K$=3 and $K$=5 are the effective control conditions where all 38 conversations were truncated; $K$=10 exceeds the maximum test benign length and serves as a no-op control.
792	
793	WildChat FPR by turn count (Part~B) provides complementary evidence: short conversations (3--10 turns, $n$=196) have \emph{lower} FPR than medium-length ones (11--15 turns, $n$=23), inconsistent with a ``short\,=\,jailbreak'' shortcut. Medium and long buckets have limited sample sizes and should be interpreted as directional evidence only.
794	
795	Padding jailbreak conversations with benign turns (Part~C) reveals an asymmetry: DD detection is resilient ($\Delta$=$-$2.5\,pp), but ActorAttack collapses ($\Delta$=$-$57.9\,pp). Padding experiments append benign turns \emph{after} the complete attack sequence; in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks. The collapse nonetheless exposes BiGRU temporal aggregation as vulnerable to attention dilution on subtle attack signals, a limitation discussed in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}.
796	
797	\section{Inference Efficiency}
798	\label{app:efficiency}
799	
800	\begin{table}[ht]
801	\centering
802	\footnotesize
803	\caption{Inference efficiency comparison. DeBERTa+BiGRU and TF-IDF+LR measured on IID test (76 conversations, avg 8.3 turns, single A100). LLM latency from separate OOD evaluation (118 conversations); cross-condition comparison is indicative only.}
804	\label{tab:efficiency}
805	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcr@{}}
806	\toprule
807	\textbf{Method} & Latency (ms) & Params & VRAM \\
808	\midrule
809	TF-IDF+LR & 0.43$\pm$0.05 & $\sim$10K & CPU \\
810	DeBERTa+BiGRU (ours) & 12.1$\pm$1.2 & 186.7M & 862\,MB \\
811	Qwen3-8B 3-shot$^{\dagger}$ & $\sim$5{,}200 & 8B & $\sim$16\,GB \\
812	\bottomrule
813	\end{tabular}
814	\vspace{-2pt}
815	{\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$Qwen3-8B latency from OOD evaluation (DD/AA, 118 conversations); not directly comparable to encoder latency measured on IID test.}
816	\end{table}
817	
818	Table~\ref{tab:efficiency} compares inference cost. On matched data (IID test), DeBERTa+BiGRU processes a conversation in 12.1\,ms, 28$\times$ slower than TF-IDF (0.43\,ms), but this speed gap purchases +71\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.972 vs.\ 0.261; Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}). LLM-as-judge approaches are orders of magnitude slower ($\sim$5.2\,s per conversation for Qwen3-8B 3-shot) while achieving substantially lower OOD detection (DD\,=\,0.566, AA\,=\,0.652; Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge}). Zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves F1\,=\,0.0 on DD OOD because it predicts all conversations as benign (Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge}). The full DeBERTa+BiGRU pipeline requires 862\,MB GPU memory, enabling deployment on consumer hardware. The 24.3$\times$ speedup reported in the main text derives from a matched-condition comparison (DisPeD 13.12\,ms vs.\ Qwen3-8B 318.53\,ms on the same GPU and data); the larger gap in Table~\ref{tab:efficiency} reflects different hardware conditions and evaluation sets.
819

          " ",
          " Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest DD OOD performance (0.818$\\pm$0.096), outperforming both vanilla (0.291$\\pm$0.065) and MLM (0.289$\\pm$0.101) by 53\\,pp. ActorAttack remains challenging under mixed-LLM conditions (9-class F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107), suggesting structurally complex attacks require larger or more diverse training data. ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark."
        ]

          " \\footnotesize",
          " \\caption{OOD F1 for 10 random binary partitions of the 9 strategy labels (each partition randomly assigns strategies to group A or B). All partitions use identical Stage-1 DAPT and Stage-2 GRU training; only the binary label mapping differs. Seed 42 (3 runs).}",
          " \\label{tab:random_partition}",
          "+\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%",
          " \\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}",
          " \\toprule",
          " \\textbf{Partition} & \\textbf{Group A} & \\textbf{IID} & \\textbf{DD OOD} & \\textbf{AA OOD} \\\\"

        "newStart": 357,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\midrule",
          " Mean$\\pm$Std &  & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & 0.964$\\pm$.109 \\\\",
          " \\bottomrule",
          "-\\end{tabular}",
          "+\\end{tabular}}",
          " \\end{table}",
          " ",
          " All 10 partitions achieve perfect IID and DD OOD detection regardless of how the 9 strategy labels are split into two groups. On ActorAttack, 9/10 partitions achieve F1\\,=\\,1.000; the sole outlier (partition~7, AA F1\\,=\\,0.636) uses a 4/5 split that isolates a specific strategy cluster. The near-universal success confirms that domain-relevant binary classification---not specific label semantics---drives cross-attack generalization on template attacks. The AA outlier is consistent with the complexity-dependent boundary observed in Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}: structurally complex attacks are sensitive to specific partition choices while template attacks are not."
        ]
      }
    ],

### User

_source line 1694 · 2026-05-24T13:39:09.804Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - **Figure 1 redraw**: Users (曹晓玉, 周俊伟) want a premium, professional Figure 1 architecture diagram for the DisPeD EMNLP 2026 paper. Must use Paper Banana only (matplotlib banned). Multiple iterations from v3 through v10b.
   - **Abstract optimization**: 曹晓玉 requested improving the abstract ("这摘要写的不好，优化一下").
   - **Paper review submission**: 曹晓玉 requested full paper review ("审一遍稿").
   - **Figure 1 replacement + recompile**: 张默涵 selected v10b and requested it be replaced in the paper and recompiled.
   - **Table overflow fixes**: 张默涵 identified Tables 11, 13, 17, 32 as having overflow/overlap issues and requested fixes.
   - **Paper PDF delivery**: 张默涵 requested the paper PDF multiple times.
   - **EMNLP 2026 ARR submission DDL: 2026-05-25** (day after current date 2026-05-24).

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **DisPeD**: Two-stage pipeline — Stage 1 adapts DeBERTa-v3-base via 7 DAPT objectives, Stage 2 uses frozen BiGRU+MLP classifier
   - **7 DAPT variants**: 9-Class (full model), Binary (label granularity), Scrambled (label correctness), MLM (supervision signal), Topic (task alignment), Wiki-MLM (domain specificity), Vanilla (Stage 2 alone)
   - **Paper Banana**: Gemini Pro-powered matplotlib code generator with VLM Critic. Requires `--vlm-model gemini-2.5-pro` and `--caption` flag. Google API key: `[REDACTED_GOOGLE_TOKEN]`
   - **Paper Banana issues**: Gemini frequently hallucinated table content (wrong variant names), added unwanted section headers, critic JSON parse errors ("Unterminated string") that default to "publication-ready"
   - **LaTeX table overflow fix**: `\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{...}` wrapping tabular environments
   - **PNG to PDF conversion**: Using Pillow's `img.convert('RGB').save(path, 'PDF', resolution=300)`

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`/tmp/fig1_intent_v9.txt`** through **`/tmp/fig1_intent_v10.txt`**: Paper Banana intent files with increasingly refined design specs
   - **`/tmp/fig1_v9d.png`**: Previous best version — correct content but user rejected as "太丑了"
   - **`/tmp/fig1_v10b.png`**: Selected version (teal minimal style) — chosen by 张默涵 ("版本2")
     - Ultra-clean geometric design, blue (#3B82F6) + teal (#0EA5E9) borders, white fills
     - Snowflake ❄️ icon for frozen encoder, circle h-states
     - Still has minor "Input: Multi-turn Conversation" header
   - **`docs/paper/figures/paper/fig1_architecture.{png,pdf}`**: Replaced with v10b, PDF converted via Pillow
   - **`docs/paper/abstract.tex`**: Rewritten abstract
     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across benign-seeming conversation turns, yet existing defenses fail on unseen attack families---Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation. What representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attacks it has never seen?

     We present DisPeD, a controlled ablation framework that isolates individual factors behind cross-attack generalization. Seven encoder variants---differing solely in their pretraining objective---share the same frozen downstream classifier, enabling direct comparison across thirteen configurations (125--355M parameters) and three OOD attack families.

     Three findings emerge. First, classification-based domain adaptation is the only robust variant across all conditions, recovering OOD detection (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31) regardless of training data composition; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Second, scrambled labels match correct labels for template-attack detection, indicating the classification objective's structural role---not label semantics---drives generalization. Third, this hierarchy reverses on human-authored data (MLM F1 0.837 vs.\ classification 0.596), revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages. Orthogonally, few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates from 68\% to 0\% with 24$\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge baselines.
     \end{abstract}

        "content": "% abstract.tex\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across benign-seeming conversation turns, yet existing defenses fail on unseen attack families---Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation. What representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attacks it has never seen?\n\nWe present DisPeD, a controlled ablation framework that isolates individual factors behind cross-attack generalization. Seven encoder variants---differing solely in their pretraining objective---share the same frozen downstream classifier, enabling direct comparison across thirteen configurations (125--355M parameters) and three OOD attack families.\n\nThree findings emerge. First, classification-based domain adaptation is the only robust variant across all conditions, recovering OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,0.97--1.0 vs.\\ vanilla 0.26--0.31) regardless of training data composition; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Second, scrambled labels match correct labels for template-attack detection, indicating the classification objective's structural role---not label semantics---drives generalization. Third, this hierarchy reverses on human-authored data (MLM F1 0.837 vs.\\ classification 0.596), revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages. Orthogonally, few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates from 68\\% to 0\\% with 24$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge baselines.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "% conclusion.tex\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_svd_subspace.pdf}\n\\caption{Test representations projected onto top-2 SVD directions. 9-class DAPT produces clear cluster separation absent in vanilla.}\n\\label{fig:svd_subspace}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Discriminative compression and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$).\nClassification DAPT compresses features into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32; Figure~\\ref{fig:svd_subspace}; Appendices~\\ref{app:representation},~\\ref{app:layerwise},~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap},~\\ref{app:tsne}), localizing to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 \\citep{zhang2021understanding}. A probe confirms sufficiency; removal degrades DD by 17\\,pp (Appendices~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe},~\\ref{app:svd_necessity}), implying that the objective's value is structural---forcing class-conditional feature organization---not encoding specific label semantics.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-condition transfer and boundaries.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond single-LLM artifacts, though the ranking reverses on human data (MLM leads; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence}). Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; distributional heterogeneity---data scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) and mixed-LLM training---erodes classification DAPT's advantage. At 355M, DAPT becomes counterproductive (vanilla AA\\,=\\,1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946) and WildChat FPR reaches 68.1\\%. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\\Delta \\leq 1$\\,pp), confirming per-turn content drives detection. Length-matched evaluation ($N$=155) shows negligible DD change ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$2.5\\,pp; Appendices~\\ref{app:length_independence},~\\ref{app:length}); the $-$57.9\\,pp ActorAttack padding collapse reflects signal dilution, not length shortcut removal (Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification DAPT recovers OOD detection at base scale (F1\\,=\\,0.97--1.0 vs.\\ vanilla 0.26--0.31), though not under mixed-LLM training on diverse attacks (ActorAttack F1\\,=\\,0.504); on human data, the hierarchy reverses (ToxicChat MLM 0.837 vs.\\ classification 0.596)---condition-specific, counterproductive at 355M. Few-shot adaptation reduces FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). This ablation framework provides a template for diagnosing when domain adaptation succeeds and fails in this safety-critical detection setting.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Synthetic data only.} All findings derive from synthetic conversations (single LLM, Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test, 3 seeds). The evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (ToxicChat; Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.\n\\item \\textbf{IID benign upper bound.} OOD F1 figures assume IID benign data; dual-OOD evaluation drops 13--14\\,pp without adaptation (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}). Few-shot benign adaptation recovers performance but is validated on only two platforms ($n \\leq 50$).\n\\item \\textbf{Scope.} Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks are addressed; single-turn token-level attacks and non-text modalities are out of scope. Cross-encoder comparisons (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) confound architecture with parameter count (184M vs.\\ 355M). The 50-sample annotation verification ($\\kappa$\\,=\\,0.78) limits evaluation of strategy characterization accuracy; however, the scrambled-label experiment demonstrates that label correctness does not affect template-attack detection. Our experiments cover base-scale encoders (125M--355M); DAPT effects may differ at larger scales, where RoBERTa-large already shows counterproductive adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 32,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 32
      }

    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 (across prefix lengths) with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization at base model scale benefits substantially from domain adaptation.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection)\\footnote{We use `disentangled' to describe our experimental methodology of isolating individual factors through controlled ablation, distinct from disentangled representation learning ($\\beta$-VAE, InfoGAN).}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation.\n\nWhile domain-adaptive pretraining generally improves downstream NLP tasks~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}, three findings in our safety-detection setting are unexpected: (i)~classification DAPT outperforms vanilla by over 60 percentage points on OOD detection (and MLM by $\\sim$50\\,pp under mixed-LLM training)---the classification objective itself, not just domain exposure, drives the OOD benefit; (ii)~scrambled labels match correct labels for template-attack detection, suggesting the classification objective's benefit is independent of label semantics; and (iii)~this hierarchy reverses entirely on human-authored data, revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages of supervised adaptation.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes (attack-side and benign-side), the first, to our knowledge, such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training; on the benign side, 10--25 conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% (WildChat, OASST2).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives, including scrambled labels, suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $|d| \\leq 0.14$ on DD, $d \\approx 0$ on FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework: domain-relevant partitioning appears necessary (topic labels fail) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removing them degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} Continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (43$\\times$ fewer parameters; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds without target-domain attack data.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 32,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 32
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. Returns step-by-step plans, identifies critical files, and considers architectural trade-offs. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. ```text
555	\section{Frozen Probing Verification}
556	\label{app:frozen_verify}
557	
558	\begin{table}[ht]
559	\centering
560	\footnotesize
561	\caption{Frozen probing vs.\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning (3-seed mean$\pm$std). 9-class: plan\_002 checkpoints; MLM: plan\_017\_mlm\_control (matched training). Frozen probing trains only BiGRU+MLP on frozen DAPT embeddings.}
562	\label{tab:frozen_verify}
563	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
564	\toprule
565	\textbf{Config} & IID & DD OOD & AA OOD & FITD OOD \\
566	\midrule
567	Frozen 9-class & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
568	Frozen MLM & .982$\pm$.016 & .993$\pm$.009 & .941$\pm$.037 & .987$\pm$.009 \\
569	E2E 9-class & 1.000$\pm$.000 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .997$\pm$.005 & --- \\
570	\bottomrule
571	\end{tabular}
572	\end{table}
573	
574	Table~\ref{tab:frozen_verify} compares frozen probing across DAPT objectives and against end-to-end fine-tuning. On easy OOD (DD, FITD), frozen MLM and frozen 9-class representations perform comparably ($\Delta \leq 1.3$\,pp). On hard OOD (ActorAttack), 9-class retains a modest advantage (0.972 vs.\ 0.941, $\Delta$\,=\,3.1\,pp) with lower variance ($\sigma$\,=\,0.026 vs.\ 0.037). Both far exceed vanilla encoders ($\leq$0.318; Table~\ref{tab:bert_proxy}). The E2E advantage over frozen 9-class is $\leq$2.5\,pp, confirming that Stage-1 representation quality is the primary source of OOD generalization.
575	
576	A striking asymmetry emerges when comparing frozen probing to the full pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}): MLM frozen representations \emph{outperform} the full MLM pipeline on AA OOD (0.941 vs.\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen exactly matches its full pipeline (0.972 vs.\ 0.972). This indicates that MLM's cross-seed instability originates in Stage-2 BiGRU training rather than Stage-1 DAPT: the MLM encoder produces adequate representations that are then degraded by unstable downstream optimization. Classification DAPT, by contrast, produces representations that remain robust through Stage-2 training, explaining its consistently lower variance (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
577	
578	This two-level structure (DAPT vs.\ no-DAPT producing a representation quality leap of +70\,pp; classification vs.\ MLM DAPT producing a stability difference) is consistent with two further observations: $\lambda$=0 matches $\lambda > 0$ during E2E fine-tuning (Table~\ref{tab:lambda}), and mean pooling matches BiGRU under E2E (Table~\ref{tab:temporal}). Once DAPT representations are strong, downstream configuration details have minimal impact.
579	
580	\subsection{Single-Turn Truncation Analysis}
581	\label{app:truncated}
582	
583	To isolate whether the ToxicChat evidence-hierarchy reversal (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}) reflects multi-turn structural dependence, we truncate each synthetic test conversation to its last user turn and re-evaluate both 9-class and JB-MLM models (3-seed mean).
584

586	\centering
587	\caption{Full-conversation vs.\ last-turn-only F1 (3-seed mean). Truncation widens the 9-class advantage, ruling out multi-turn structure as the driver of the ToxicChat reversal.}

594	IID & Last turn & .991 & .894 \\

56	\end{table}
57	
58	Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
59	On OOD DD, vanilla drops to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 and worsens with more turns (0.628$\to$0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying benign embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}. MLM recovers to $\geq$0.97 at $K \leq 5$ without labels but is unstable at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115); 9-class maintains 0.997$\pm$0.005.
60	On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses (0.261) while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026, a 71\,pp gap confirming domain-adapted representations capture beyond-lexical features.
61	Seed-level results confirm consistent separation (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}; bootstrap CIs non-overlapping, permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all F1\,$\geq$\,0.990).
62

64	\centering
65	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}

68	\end{figure}
69

135	\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}
136	\label{sec:cross_encoder}
137	
138	\begin{table}[t]
139	\centering
140	\small
141	\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}
142	\label{tab:cross_encoder}
143	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
144	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
145	\toprule
146	\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\
147	\midrule
148	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\[2pt]
149	Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
150	MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 \\
151	9-class & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.972$\pm$.026} \\
152	\midrule
153	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\[2pt]
154	Vanilla & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
155	MLM & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
156	9-class & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.939$\pm$.046} \\
157	\midrule
158	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
159	Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
160	MLM & .884$\pm$.065 & .874$\pm$.014 \\
161	9-class & \textbf{.987$\pm$.004} & .946$\pm$.057 \\
162	\bottomrule
163	\end{tabular}}
164	\end{table}
165	
166	To test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
167	On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
168	
169	RoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the DAPT advantage on ActorAttack (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946; Table~\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), revealing a difficulty threshold: base-scale encoders require DAPT for all attacks, while sufficient capacity renders it unnecessary. A BERT-base proxy confirms DAPT, not architecture, drives generalization (Appendix~\ref{app:bert_proxy}).
170	
171	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
172	\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}
173	
174	All preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).
175	Classification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\pm$0.101). On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, detection degrades to chance level (9-class F1\,=\,0.504$\pm$0.107, indistinguishable from majority baseline at $n$\,=\,3).
176	Classification supervision thus generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not (Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}).
177	
178	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
179	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
180	\label{sec:human_data}
181	
182	Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
183

185	\centering

Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}",

30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack families underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.
36	
37	\section{Full Cross-Attack Results by Prefix}
38	\label{sec:full_prefix}

          " \\caption{Frozen probing vs.\\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). 9-class: plan\\_002 checkpoints; MLM: plan\\_017\\_mlm\\_control (matched training). Frozen probing trains only BiGRU+MLP on frozen DAPT embeddings.}",
          " \\label{tab:frozen_verify}",
          "+\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%",
          " \\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}",
          " \\toprule",
          " \\textbf{Config} & IID & DD OOD & AA OOD & FITD OOD \\\\"

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Frozen MLM & .982$\\pm$.016 & .993$\\pm$.009 & .941$\\pm$.037 & .987$\\pm$.009 \\\\",
          " E2E 9-class & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .997$\\pm$.005 & --- \\\\",
          " \\bottomrule",
          "-\\end{tabular}",
          "+\\end{tabular}}",
          " \\end{table}",
          " ",
          " Table~\\ref{tab:frozen_verify} compares frozen probing across DAPT objectives and against end-to-end fine-tuning. On easy OOD (DD, FITD), frozen MLM and frozen 9-class representations perform comparably ($\\Delta \\leq 1.3$\\,pp). On hard OOD (ActorAttack), 9-class retains a modest advantage (0.972 vs.\\ 0.941, $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.1\\,pp) with lower variance ($\\sigma$\\,=\\,0.026 vs.\\ 0.037). Both far exceed vanilla encoders ($\\leq$0.318; Table~\\ref{tab:bert_proxy}). The E2E advantage over frozen 9-class is $\\leq$2.5\\,pp, confirming that Stage-1 representation quality is the primary source of OOD generalization."
        ]
      }
    ],

```json
{
  "type": "queue-operation",
  "operation": "enqueue",
  "timestamp": "2026-05-24T13:48:30.261Z",
  "sessionId": "d0343bc5-105f-48da-b75b-2236f7e1bb2d",
  "content": "[审稿报告]\nOverall: 3.0 (Findings)\n\n# Round 23 审稿报告\n\n## Summary\n\n本文对 DeBERTa-v3 在多轮越狱检测任务上的 7 种领域自适应预训练（DAPT）策略进行了系统性消融研究，建立了从 vanilla continued pretraining 到 9-class persuasion strategy classification 的性能梯度（\"evidence hierarchy\"），并发现 scrambled labels 在 template attacks 上与正确标签等效。论文还报告了 ToxicChat 人类数据上 hierarchy 反转、RoBERTa-large 规模下 DAPT 反效果等 boundary conditions。实验设计全面（7 variants × 2 evaluation axes × 3 seeds × 2 encoders），但核心叙事在真实数据场景中受到严重削弱。\n\n## Strengths\n\n**S1: 系统性消融设计。** 7 种 DAPT 变体构成完整的因子分解光谱（vanilla → Wiki-MLM → JB-MLM → topic → binary → scrambled → 9-class），每个步骤隔离一个实验因素。这种设计在安全检测领域罕见。\n\n**S2: 两轴评估框架。** 将泛化能力分解为 attack-side OOD（跨攻击家族）和 benign-side OOD（跨 benign 分布）两个正交维度，结构清晰，便于定位失败模式。\n\n**S3: Boundary conditions 的诚实报告。** ToxicChat 反转、RoBERTa-large DAPT 反效果、ActorAttack 上 scrambled ≠ 9-class 等 negative/boundary results 均在论文中呈现，没有选择性报告。\n\n**S4: Scrambled label 等价性发现。** 在 template attacks（DangerousDialogues, FITD）上 scrambled labels 与正确 9-class labels 的 TOST 等价检验通过（p<0.05, |d|≤0.14），这一发现对理解 DAPT 机制有启示——classification objective 的结构性角色比标签语义更重要（在适用范围内）。\n\n**S5: 实验基础设施质量。** 10-seed TOST、BCa bootstrap CI、CKA/SVD 表征分析、多粒度长度控制实验等统计和分析手段丰富，方法论意识强。\n\n**S6: 实用部署价值。** 与 LLM-as-judge 相比 24-43× 推理加速，few-shot benign adaptation 仅需 10-25 条对话即可将 WildChat FPR 从 68% 降至 0-1.5%，具备实际部署潜力。\n\n## Weaknesses\n\n### Must-Fix\n\n**MF1 [共识]: ToxicChat hierarchy 反转严重削弱核心叙事。**\nToxicChat 是论文唯一的人类数据评估（Table 5, Section 4.6），MLM 以 F1=0.837 大幅超越 classification F1=0.596。论文核心叙事 \"classification-based DAPT is the most robust variant\" 在人类数据上质的反转。论文在 Section 5 和 Conclusion 各用一句话带过，处理深度与问题严重性不匹配。综合 WildChat 68.1% FPR（Section 4.8），论文的核心 hierarchy 在**所有真实数据场景**中均失效或严重退化：\n- 合成 OOD 数据：hierarchy 成立\n- ToxicChat（人类数据，单轮）：hierarchy 反转\n- WildChat（人类数据，多轮 benign）：68.1% FPR\n\n需要增加对 ToxicChat 反转的深入分析（至少 1-2 段），并在 Conclusion 中给出条件性结论而非一句话概括。[R1-N3 ↔ R2-N2, 两位 AGREE]\n\n**MF2 [共识]: Abstract 中 \"label semantics\" claim 范围未限定。**\nAbstract 称 \"indicating the classification objective's structural role—not label semantics—drives generalization\"，但 TOST 在 ActorAttack 上失败（p=0.183, d=0.44, Appendix L Table 16）。\"Generalization\" 未限定为 template attacks，读者可能误解为适用于所有攻击类型。Section 4.3 正文有正确限定，但 Abstract 缺失。需修改为 \"drives generalization **on template attacks**\" 或 \"**for structurally consistent attack families**\"。[R1-N2 ↔ R2 cross AGREE]\n\n**MF3 [共识，严重程度有分歧]: 核心 evidence hierarchy 增量新颖性定位过高。**\nClassification DAPT 优于 vanilla/MLM 在 Gururangan et al. (2020) 的 DAPT 框架下是 largely predictable——该文已系统证明 task-adaptive pretraining 在多个 NLP 任务上优于 vanilla continued pretraining。论文将此结果标记为三大 \"unexpected findings\" 之一（Section 1, page 2），novelty framing 有拔高之嫌。论文的真正贡献在于因子分解的定量化（domain exposure vs classification supervision 的贡献量级、boundary conditions、scrambled equivalence），而非 hierarchy 排序本身。建议将 finding (i) 重新定位为 \"quantifying the magnitude and conditions\" 而非 \"unexpected\"。[R2-N1 critical + R1 cross AGREE but prefers major. Director 判定: Must-Fix for framing]\n\n### Should-Fix\n\n**SF1 [分歧裁定]: 对话长度混淆——现有控制充分但缺直接 baseline。**\nBenign 12-20 turns vs jailbreak 3-10 turns 的长度差异是潜在混淆因素。论文已提供截断验证（Appendix V, Table 32 Part A: 截断后 FPR=0.000）、length-matched 子集（N=155, ΔF1=-2.5pp）、length stratification（Appendix U, Table 31: 无系统性退化）。R2 认为这些控制已充分排除长度 shortcut；R1 认为 turn-count-only baseline F1 是更直接的反驳。**Director 裁定: Should-Fix**——现有控制合理但非决定性，一个简单的 turn-count classifier 可一步封堵此质疑。[R1-N1 Must-Fix vs R2 Should-Fix]\n\n**SF2 [分歧裁定]: WildChat 68.1% FPR——合成数据训练的根本局限。**\nR1 将此视为跨域泛化失败；R2 指出论文的 \"cross-attack generalization\" 始终限定在攻击家族维度而非域泛化。R2 的论证在技术上正确——abstract 已标注 few-shot adaptation，没有声称 zero-shot 跨域泛化。**Director 裁定: Should-Fix**——68.1% FPR 不构成 soundness 问题，但暴露了合成 benign 训练数据的系统性偏差，应在 Discussion 中更明确讨论。需第二个人类多轮数据源（如 ShareGPT 或 LMSYS-Chat）的验证。[R1-N3 Must-Fix vs R2 Minor]\n\n**SF3 [共识]: TOST δ=5pp 缺乏 practical significance 论证。**\n在 F1>0.95 的区间，5pp 差异意味着 miss rate 的数倍变化——如果 5pp 在部署场景下有实际意义，用它做等价阈值则自相矛盾。应补充 domain-specific practical significance 论证或 δ=3pp sensitivity analysis。[R1-W1 + R2 AGREE]\n\n**SF4 [共识]: Topic 变体 5 vs 9 类别数量混淆。**\nTopic variant 用 5-class 分类而 9-class variant 用 9 categories（Table 1）。Topic 的失败可能因为 5 classes 不足以产生足够的 discriminative compression，而非因为 topic labels 与 jailbreak detection 无关。一个 9→5 class merging 控制实验成本低且能直接回答这个问题。[R1-W3 + R2 AGREE]\n\n**SF5 [共识]: RoBERTa-large DAPT \"有害\" 机制未解释。**\nSection 4.4 和 Section 5 解释了为什么大模型不需要 DAPT（vanilla capacity 足够），但 9-class DAPT 在 AA 上 F1 从 1.000→0.946 表明 DAPT 是 actively harmful，不仅仅是 unnecessary。可能的机制（over-specialization、catastrophic forgetting）未被区分。CKA 分析（Appendix S, Figure 6）可能提供线索但未被讨论。[R1-W4 + R2-W4 AGREE]\n\n**SF6 [共识]: 1000 样本性能反常下降未解释。**\nAppendix I (Table 9/10) 报告 1000 样本时的性能下降，论文归因于 distributional heterogeneity，但无实验验证。应报告 1000 样本的 training loss 曲线、data composition 分析或 seed 一致性验证。[R1-W5 + R2 AGREE]\n\n**SF7 [共识]: \"Evidence hierarchy\" 术语缺乏正式定义。**\n从 Section 1 (page 1-2) 开始频繁使用但首次出现时无明确定义。读者需从上下文推断含义。R1 进一步指出该术语暗示超出经验排序的理论地位，建议改为 \"performance ranking\"。应在首次使用处加入操作性定义。[R2-W1 + R1 AGREE]\n\n**SF8 [共识]: Conclusion \"reusable template\" claim 超出实证范围。**\nSection 6 声称 \"This ablation framework provides a template for diagnosing when domain adaptation succeeds and fails in this safety-critical detection setting\"，但实证基础仅限于单一任务、单一数据源、单一 encoder 家族。应限定为 \"template for future controlled studies of domain adaptation in safety-critical NLP tasks\"。[R2-W3 + R1 AGREE]\n\n**SF9 [共识]: BiGRU vs mean pooling 讨论不足。**\nAppendix T (Table 30) 显示 frozen probing 下 BiGRU 和 mean pooling 差异在 CI 范围内，E2E fine-tuning 下均收敛到 F1=1.000。这暗示 temporal order 在充分表征质量下非必需，与论文将 BiGRU 作为 Stage 2 核心组件的设计选择相关。应在正文明确说明 BiGRU 可被 mean pooling 替代且不影响检测性能。[R1-W3 + R2-W4 AGREE]\n\n**SF10 [共识]: 策略标注质量验证不足。**\n50 样本验证 9 个类别，部分类别仅 2-5 个验证样本，κ=0.78 在此粒度下可靠性不足。Scrambled 实验缓解了对主要检测结论的影响，但 Appendix Y 策略预测准确率（90.1%）受标注质量直接限制。建议增加验证样本至≥100 并报告 per-class κ。[R1-W4 + R2 AGREE]\n\n### Minor\n\n**M1: Abstract 句法模糊。** \"this hierarchy reverses\" 后的修饰对象不清晰，需要重写以消除歧义。[R2, 跨轮持续存在]\n\n**M2: Scrambled ≈ 9-class 发现的叙事定位。** 该发现可能是论文最具洞察力的结果，但当前论文将其作为 hierarchy 排序的补充讨论，而非核心贡献。建议在 Section 5 增加段落讨论 classification objective structure vs label semantics 的机制意涵。[R1-W2 ↔ R2-W2]\n\n## Reviewer Consensus\n\n以下关键问题两位 Reviewer 完全一致：\n1. ToxicChat hierarchy 反转是论文最大的 narrative concern（MF1）\n2. Abstract \"label semantics\" claim 需限定到 template attacks（MF2）\n3. Scrambled ≈ 9-class 在 ActorAttack 上不成立（d=0.44, TOST p=0.183）\n4. \"Reusable template\" overclaim 需限定范围（SF8）\n5. RoBERTa-large DAPT 反转缺乏机制解释（SF5）\n6. BiGRU 可被 mean pooling 替代（SF9）\n7. Overall 3.0 (Findings) 评分\n\n## Reviewer Divergence\n\n**分歧 1: 长度混淆严重程度** — R1 列为 Must-Fix（turn-count shortcut 可能性未排除）；R2 列为 Should-Fix（Appendix U/V 的截断、length-matched、stratification 实验已充分控制）。Director 裁定 Should-Fix：现有控制合理，但 turn-count-only baseline 可一步封堵。\n\n**分歧 2: WildChat 68.1% FPR 严重程度** — R1 列为 Must-Fix（跨域泛化声明严重削弱）；R2 列为 Minor wording（论文从未声称跨域泛化，attack-side 和 benign-side 是正交轴）。Director 裁定 Should-Fix：R2 技术上正确但 68.1% FPR 暴露合成数据训练的系统性偏差，需更充分讨论。\n\n**分歧 3 (minor): Scrambled \"counter-intuitive\" framing** — R2 认为在 discriminative compression 框架下可预期；R1 认为对 NLP 社区多数读者仍具 counter-intuitive 成分（random labels 在标准 NLP 文献中被视为 noise injection 而非有效训练信号）。Director 判断 R1 更准确——scrambled ≈ 9-class 确有 counter-intuitive 价值，但 ActorAttack 例外需在正文突出。\n\n## Questions\n\n**Q1:** 作者能否提供一个 turn-count-only classifier 的 F1 作为 sanity check？这是一个零成本实验，可以彻底排除长度 shortcut 质疑。\n\n**Q2:** ToxicChat 反转的根因是 single-turn 特性还是 distributional properties？作者能否将 ToxicChat 数据用多轮格式包装（padding with neutral turns）测试 classification DAPT 是否恢复？\n\n**Q3:** RoBERTa-large 上 9-class DAPT 导致 AA F1 从 1.000 降至 0.946 的机制是什么？CKA 分析（Figure 6）能否提供线索？\n\n**Q4:** 1000 样本性能下降是否在 3 个 seeds 中一致出现？训练 loss 曲线是否显示过拟合迹象？\n\n**Q5:** 如果将 9-class 策略标签合并为 5 个类别，性能是否下降至 topic variant 水平？这可以区分类别数量和标签相关性的各自贡献。\n\n## Missing References\n\n- **Galinkin & Sablotny (2024)**. \"Improved LLM Jailbreak Detection via Pretrained Embeddings.\" arXiv:2412.01547. 最直接相关的已发表工作——同样使用预训练 encoder embeddings 进行越狱检测。两位 Reviewer 共识应补充。[R22 遗留]\n- **Perez and Wang (2022)** red teaming 工作。R1 建议补充，R2 指出论文可能已引用 Perez et al. (2022)，需确认是否为同一工作。\n\n## External Review (Stanford Agentic Reviewer)\n\n未请求。\n\n## Visual Layout Check（非评分项）\n\n视觉审查已完成（3 warnings, 9 info items）。\n\n**Warnings:**\n1. **[Page 9] Conclusion 溢出**: Section 6 (Conclusion) 正文溢出约 3-4 行到第 9 页顶部，之后才是 Limitations。EMNLP 2026 正文限 8 页（Limitations 不计入）。**注意**: Round 20 曾将类似报告标记为 FALSE POSITIVE（\"Sections 1-6 在 p1-p8\"），本轮视觉审查重新检测到溢出——可能是后续修改导致的回归。建议作者确认 Section 6 最后一行是否在第 8 页内，若确实溢出需压缩 Discussion/Conclusion。\n2. **[Pages 3-5] 纯文本区间过长**: Related Work (p3) + Experiments Setup (p5) 均为纯文本，中间仅 p4 右栏有 Table 1，连续 3 页缺乏视觉锚点。建议将 Figure 2 或某关键表格前移。\n3. **[Page 15] Figure 5 heatmap 标注过小**: 三个 heatmap 并排，cell 内数值在 150dpi 下偏小，打印可能难辨。\n\n**Info 亮点**: Figure 1 架构图位置好（p2 顶部）、全文表格 booktabs 风格统一、未发现 LaTeX 渲染错误或未解析引用。正文 display item 密度 1.1/页，符合实证论文标准。\n\n## Overall Assessment\n\n**评分: 3.0 (Findings)**\n**置信度: 4/5 (High)**\n\n本文是一项全面、系统的消融研究，实验设计质量高，分析工具丰富。但以下因素阻碍其达到 Conference (4.0) 水平：\n\n1. **核心叙事在真实数据上失效**: ToxicChat hierarchy 反转 + WildChat 68.1% FPR 表明论文的 \"evidence hierarchy\" 仅在合成数据生态系统内成立。这不是 soundness 缺陷（论文报告了这些结果），但严重限制了结论的实际影响力。\n2. **方法新颖性有限**: 论文本质上是将已知的 DAPT 范式（Gururangan et al., 2020）应用于新领域，方法层面无创新。其贡献在于 empirical findings（scrambled equivalence、boundary conditions），但这些发现中的核心一条（classification > MLM ranking）可从先行工作预期。\n3. **Claims 多处超出实证范围**: \"reusable template\"（单一任务）、\"label semantics don't matter\"（仅 template attacks）、\"unexpected\"（可预期）等表述需要系统性收窄。\n\n**为何不低于 3.0**: 实验执行质量高，因子分解设计在安全检测领域有参考价值，scrambled label equivalence 和 boundary conditions 的发现对社区有实用启示。论文作为 Findings paper 的贡献足够。\n\n**为何不高于 3.0**: 核心叙事的真实数据泛化失败是结构性问题而非措辞问题——即使完美修改 framing，论文的主要 take-away 仍然是 \"DAPT hierarchy exists but only in synthetic settings\"，这限制了 impact。方法新颖性不足以支撑 Conference 推荐。\n\n## Detailed Comments\n\n### Abstract\n- \"indicating the classification objective's structural role—not label semantics—drives generalization\" 需限定为 template attacks（MF2）\n- \"this hierarchy reverses\" 句法模糊，修饰对象不清（M1）\n- 三大 \"unexpected findings\" 中的 finding (i) 应降格为 \"quantifying the magnitude\"（MF3）\n\n### Section 1 (Introduction)\n- \"Evidence hierarchy\" 首次使用处需操作性定义（SF7）\n- 三大贡献的 novelty framing 需调整——contribution #1 从 \"unexpected\" 改为 \"systematic quantification\"（MF3）\n\n### Section 3 (Method)\n- BiGRU 选择理由需补充说明——正文应明确 mean pooling 可替代（SF9）\n\n### Section 4.3 (Evidence Hierarchy)\n- 正文有 ActorAttack d=0.44 的限定，但 abstract 缺失——需保持一致（MF2）\n- Topic 5-class vs 9-class 类别数量混淆需讨论或补充控制实验（SF4）\n\n### Section 4.4 (Scale Effects)\n- \"DAPT unnecessary at 355M\" 需区分 \"unnecessary\" vs \"harmful\"，讨论 CKA 线索（SF5）\n\n### Section 4.6 (ToxicChat)\n- 需从 1 句话扩展为 1-2 段深入分析，隔离 single-turn vs distributional 因素（MF1）\n\n### Section 4.7 (Data Scale)\n- 1000 样本下降需补充 seed 一致性和 training loss 证据（SF6）\n\n### Section 5 (Discussion)\n- 增加 scrambled equivalence 的机制讨论段落（M2）\n- WildChat FPR 作为合成数据训练局限的讨论需更充分（SF2）\n\n### Section 6 (Conclusion)\n- \"reusable template\" 限定范围（SF8）\n- ToxicChat 反转的条件性结论需加深（MF1）\n- 检查是否溢出到 page 9（Visual Check Warning #1）\n\n### Appendix\n- TOST δ=5pp 补充 practical significance 论证（SF3）\n- 策略标注验证样本量增加至≥100，报告 per-class κ（SF10）\n- 确认 Galinkin & Sablotny (2024) 和 Perez and Wang (2022) 引用状态\n---\n\n## ⚠️ 处理协议(强制,逐条执行,禁止简略)\n\n**收到上面的审稿报告后必须按以下顺序处理**:\n\n1. **逐条编号通读**: 把报告里所有问题、修改建议一项一项列出来(R1, R2, R3...),\n   即使有的看起来重复或次要也要单独保留 — 不允许漏任何一条。\n\n2. **每条给出详细修复方案**:\n   - 补实验: 写明实验配置(baseline / 超参 / seed / dataset / metric) + 预估 GPU 时长\n   - 改论文: 写明改哪一节、改写思路、新引用是否需要补\n   - 改 framing / 措辞: 写明原文 + 改写后版本\n   - 写 rebuttal: 写明论点和支撑证据\n   - 拒绝: 写明拒绝理由(reviewer 误读 / 超出 scope / 已有反驳证据)\n\n3. **必须调 `post_to_reviewer` 让 Research Reviewer 补充意见**:\n   把完整审稿报告 + 你的逐条初步方案打包发给 Reviewer, 让 ta 独立审视有没有\n   遗漏、方案可行性、技术细节是否合理。**这一步不可省** — Reviewer 是你的\n   独立审查员, 他对每条审稿意见的判断和你互补。\n\n4. **收到 Reviewer 补充后综合定稿**: 把 reviewer 反馈合并进你的 Action Matrix,\n   再安排 Main Agent 执行(补实验 / 改论文)或向用户确认 venue 变更。\n   **对 CRITICAL/WARNING 级的修复指令, 必须要求 Agent 修改完成后把修后段落 +\n   原始审稿意见发给 Reviewer 做验证 — Reviewer 以审稿人视角判断修复是否充分,\n   返回 PASS 才算完成。** 不验证就标 resolved 是\"修了但没修对\"的根源。\n\n5. **同步 Issue Tracker**: Action Matrix 定稿后, 用 `read_local_file` 读\n   `projects/{project}/artifacts/review_issues.yaml`, 把本轮处理的 issue 标\n   `in_progress`、Reviewer 已确认修复的标 `resolved`, 调 `update_review_issues`\n   写回。这是跨轮次的修复追踪 — Continue 模式审稿要靠这个表识别复发问题。\n\n6. **区分写作问题和 evidence 问题**: 对每条 CRITICAL/WARNING 级审稿意见,\n   先判断: \"如果论文写得完美无缺(措辞零瑕疵),这个问题还存在吗?\"\n   - 是 → 这是 evidence 问题, 不能只改论文。必须补实验/补分析/降级 claim\n   - 否 → 这是写作问题, 可以通过改论文修复\n   审稿人说\"not convincing / insufficient evidence / lacks support / confound /\n   reliability / sample size\"时, 基本都是 evidence 问题 — 不要用加 hedging 应付。\n\n7. **检查复发问题**: 在制定修复方案之前, 先 `read_local_file` 读\n   `projects/{project}/artifacts/review_issues.yaml`, 找出本轮审稿意见中\n   哪些是之前已经出现过的老问题。对复发问题重点关注:\n   - 查看上轮用了什么方法修复(action_items 中记录了)\n   - 如果上轮已经用写作手段修复过但审稿人仍然 flag → 写作手段对这个问题无效,\n     必须换路径(补实验 / 补分析 / 降级 claim 位置)\n   - 在 Action Matrix 中标注 \"[复发 × N 轮]\", 并说明为什么这次的修复方案\n     和之前不同\n\n8. **claim 校准是双向的**: 实验结果超预期时 claim 可以升级(扩大 scope / 去掉\n   hedging / 提升到 headline), 实验结果不及预期时 claim 应该降级。降级 claim\n   不是降级论文 — 把一个 evidence 不足的 finding 从 headline 移到 discussion,\n   反而让论文整体更可信。\n\n9. **向 A 群汇报**: Action Matrix 定稿后,用 `director_reply_to_user` 向用户汇报:\n   - 审稿整体评估(系统性问题有哪些、严重程度)\n   - Action Matrix 摘要(补哪些实验、改哪些章节、写哪些 rebuttal)\n   - 预估时间线\n   - 需要用户决策的事项(如有)\n\n**禁止**:\n- 跳过第 3 步直接派 Main Agent\n- 把多条审稿意见合并成一条 action\n- 对低 priority 意见用\"暂不处理\"一笔带过而不给出明确理由\n- 整体性\"已知悉\"回复\n- 对 evidence 问题只用写作手段修复(第 6 条)\n- 对复发问题重复使用已失败的修复方法(第 7 条)\n"
}
